 304: 
 305: \begin{remark}[Exact residual arithmetic interface]
 306: \label{rem:exact-average-open-interface}
 307: The \suppref{Corollary ``Theta-refined connected support entropy''} and
 308: Theorem~\ref{thm:minimal-generator-growth} determine the contribution of the
 309: multiplicity-free minimal-cover model used here: $\log2/4$ is its exact
 310: quadratic coefficient, the fixed-choice rank-pure sector supplies its
 311: theta-refined lower terms, and the private-coordinate upper bound retains the
 312: term $\omega(n)\log R(n)$. The rank partition used in
 313: The rank-window argument in the \suppref{Lemma ``Arithmetic tests for the
 314: three-coordinate atom families''} applies verbatim to every support size:
 315: prime-atom families are finite disjoint unions of the rank
 316: classes $\Pi_\alpha(d)$, while ladder families are singletons. Thus
 317: Conjectures~\ref{hyp:normal-window} and
 318: \ref{hyp:mean-window} ask precisely for upper distributional control of
 319: exact-rank prime multiplicities in those rank windows.
 320: 
 321: The pointwise estimate \eqref{eq:pointwise-exact-rank-bound} proves only the
 322: consequence obtained by combining the fibotomic size identity with the
 323: divisibility of the exact-rank radical and the congruences
 324: $p\equiv\pm1\pmod d$. The \suppref{remark ``Cumulative rank estimates do not
 325: imply (H2)''} shows that even its
 326: Euler-factor scale does not by itself imply either rank-window conjecture. The
 327: manuscript does not prove that these inputs exhaust the information available
 328: from fibotomic arithmetic, or that
 329: \eqref{eq:fibotomic-rank-entropy} is optimal within any information class. Such
 330: an optimality theorem would require, at minimum, a precise axiom class of
 331: admissible information and a realizable extremal model satisfying those
 332: axioms. Neither is supplied here. Whether stronger unconditional dispersion or
 333: concentration consequences follow from fibotomic arithmetic is therefore an
 334: open interface.
 335: 
 336: The closest distribution theorems do not close this interface.  In the
 337: notation of a nondegenerate Lucas sequence $U$ with nonsquare discriminant
 338: $\Delta$ and squarefree discriminant part $\Delta_0$, Sanna's Theorem~1.1
 339: \cite{Sanna2022RankDivisibility} gives, unconditionally, an asymptotic formula
 340: for
 341: \[
 342:  \#\{p\le x:d\mid\rho_U(p)\}
 343: \]
 344: when $d$ is odd, with $3\nmid d$ if $\Delta_0=-3$, and
 345: $x>\exp(Be^{8\omega(d)}d^8)$.  The condition counted is
 346: $d\mid\rho_U(p)$, not the exact-rank condition $\rho_U(p)=d$; for example,
 347: $3\mid\alpha(17)=9$ although $\alpha(17)\ne3$.  Sanna's Theorem~1.1 on the
 348: index of appearance \cite{Sanna2024IndexAppearance} instead assumes GRH and,
 349: for a fixed positive integer $t$, counts primes having
 350: $\iota_U(p)=t$ uniformly for $x>t^3$.  In the Fibonacci case,
 351: $\alpha(p)=d$ and $\iota_F(p)=t$ imply
 352: \[
 353:  p=td+\left(\frac5p\right)=td\pm1.
 354: \]
 355: Taking $x$ on the scale of this candidate prime restricts that theorem to
 356: $t^2\ll d$, and its cumulative fixed-index count still does not isolate the
 357: single rank $d$.  Thus neither result controls the growing exact fibers
 358: $\Pi_\alpha(d)$ required in \eqref{eq:visible-single-rank-interface}.
 359: 
 360: The primitive-divisor theorems of Carmichael and Bilu--Hanrot--Voutier give
 361: existence in the Fibonacci setting. Granville proves an odd-multiplicity
 362: primitive-factor theorem for a class of second-order Lucas recurrences with
 363: $c\equiv2\pmod4$ and explicitly leaves the odd-$b,c$ case, including the
 364: Fibonacci recurrence $b=c=1$, outside that theorem
 365: \cite{Granville2012PrimitivePrimeFactors}. Thus it is a neighboring
 366: Lucas-sequence result, not a Fibonacci multiplicity input here. Kiss treats
 367: related Lucas-sequence primitive-divisor questions
 368: \cite{Kiss1988PrimitiveDivisors}. Hong proves that, for each fixed positive
 369: integer $\kappa$ and all sufficiently large $n$ with an explicit threshold,
 370: $F_n$ has a primitive divisor at least $(\kappa+1)n-1$
 371: \cite{Hong2025BigPrimitiveDivisors}. These results concern existence, parity of
 372: one primitive factor's multiplicity in the stated Lucas class, or the size of
 373: one Fibonacci primitive divisor; they do not provide the required average
 374: dispersion or critical concentration estimates for Fibonacci exact-rank prime
 375: multiplicities in visible divisor windows. The
 376: Hardy--Ramanujan and
 377: Tur\'an theorems control the additive function $\omega(n)$ but not the prime
 378: factorization of $F_d$ across exact apparition ranks. Likewise, the
 379: Erd\H{o}s--Wintner theorem \cite{ErdosWintner1939Additive} concerns limiting
 380: distributions of additive arithmetic functions, whereas $L(n)$ and $R(n)$
 381: are not additive. No implication from RH or ERH to either rank-window
 382: conjecture is presently known. Accordingly, the normal and mean equivalents
 383: \eqref{eq:conditional-normal-order} and
 384: \eqref{eq:conditional-mean-order} are conditional; the unconditional
 385: statements are \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order} and
 386: \eqref{eq:mean-logarithmic-growth}, together with the stronger connected lower
 387: bound in the \suppref{Corollary ``Theta-refined connected support entropy''}.
 388: None of these estimates implies that the disconnected part of $\mathcal M_n$
 389: is negligible; the strictly stronger blockwise hypothesis needed for that
 390: conclusion is isolated in the \suppref{Theorem ``Blockwise criterion for
 391: connected dominance''}.
 392: \end{remark}
 393: 

 succeeded in 188ms:
   1: \section{Support entropy and the unresolved arithmetic multiplicity}
   2: \label{sec:growth}
   3: %% ====================================================================
   4: 
   5: Write $\Omega(N)=\sum_p\nu_p(N)$ for the number of prime factors of $N$
   6: counted with multiplicity. Set $R(2)=1$, consistently with
   7: $\mathcal M_2=\varnothing$. For $n\ge3$ define the maximal atomic rank-window
   8: multiplicity
   9: \begin{equation}\label{eq:rank-window-multiplicity}
  10:  R(n):=\max\!\left\{1,
  11:  \max_{\varnothing\ne I\subseteq J\subseteq\mathcal P(n)}
  12:        \#\mathcal A_{I,J}(n)\right\}.
  13: \end{equation}
  14: By the prime--ladder alternative, the nonsingleton families counted here are the
  15: exact-rank windows $P_J(n)$, whereas every ladder family contributes at most
  16: one atom. Thus $R(n)$ records the maximal atomic-family multiplicity retained
  17: by the counting bounds below. It is an exact parameter for those bounds, but
  18: it is not asserted to be an information-theoretically complete invariant of
  19: the arithmetic outside the support combinatorics.
  20: 
  21: We first remove the contribution caused solely by collecting several exact
  22: rank classes in one support window. For $d\mid n$, write
  23: \[
  24:  T_n(d):=\{\ell\in\mathcal P(n):
  25:  \nu_\ell(d)=\nu_\ell(n)\}.
  26: \]
  27: 
  28: \begin{theorem}[Mean rank-window deaggregation]
  29: \label{thm:mean-rank-window-deaggregation}
  30: Put
  31: \[
  32:  a(d):=\#\Pi_\alpha(d),\qquad
  33:  \mathcal D^+(n):=\{d\mid n:T_n(d)\ne\varnothing\},
  34: \]
  35: and
  36: \[
  37:  A^*(n):=\max\left\{1,\max_{d\in\mathcal D^+(n)}a(d)\right\}.
  38: \]
  39: Then, as $x\to\infty$,
  40: \begin{equation}\label{eq:mean-window-deaggregation}
  41:  \sum_{3\le n\le x}\omega(n)\log R(n)
  42:  =\sum_{3\le n\le x}\omega(n)\log A^*(n)
  43:  +O(x\log\log x).
  44: \end{equation}
  45: Consequently the mean sparse-window hypothesis \textup{(H2)} is equivalent to
  46: \begin{equation}\label{eq:visible-single-rank-interface}
  47:  \sum_{3\le n\le x}\omega(n)
  48:  \log\max\left\{1,
  49:  \max_{\substack{d\mid n\\T_n(d)\ne\varnothing}}
  50:  \#\Pi_\alpha(d)\right\}
  51:  =o\bigl(x(\log\log x)^2\bigr).
  52: \end{equation}
  53: \end{theorem}
  54: 
  55: \begin{proof}
  56: For nonempty $J\subseteq\mathcal P(n)$, let
  57: \[
  58:  W_J(n):=\{d\mid n:T_n(d)=J\}.
  59: \]
  60: If $n=\prod_\ell\ell^{e_\ell}$, then
  61: \begin{equation}\label{eq:support-window-size}
  62:  \#W_J(n)=\prod_{\ell\notin J}e_\ell.
  63: \end{equation}
  64: The prime atoms in the family with essential and full supports $(J,J)$ form
  65: the disjoint union
  66: \[
  67:  \coprod_{d\in W_J(n)}\Pi_\alpha(d),
  68: \]
  69: because a prime has a unique rank of apparition. By the prime--ladder
  70: alternative and rank-lifting formula proved in the \suppref{section
  71: ``Low-support classifications''}, every remaining effective atomic family is
  72: a ladder family with singleton essential support. Once that essential
  73: coordinate $\ell$ is fixed, the underlying prime is $\ell$, and the
  74: full-exponent equation determines at most one ladder exponent. Therefore, with
  75: \[
  76:  P(n):=\max_{\varnothing\ne J\subseteq\mathcal P(n)}
  77:        \sum_{d\in W_J(n)}a(d),
  78: \]
  79: one has
  80: \begin{equation}\label{eq:prime-window-versus-R}
  81:  P(n)\le R(n)\le P(n)+1.
  82: \end{equation}
  83: Every $d\in\mathcal D^+(n)$ belongs to one of the windows above. Hence
  84: \[
  85:  A^*(n)\le R(n)
  86:  \le1+A^*(n)\max_J\#W_J(n)
  87:  \le1+A^*(n)\prod_{\ell^{e_\ell}\parallel n}e_\ell.
  88: \]
  89: Writing
  90: \[
  91:  h(n):=\sum_{\ell^{e_\ell}\parallel n}\log e_\ell,
  92: \]
  93: and using $A^*(n)\ge1$ gives
  94: \begin{equation}\label{eq:pointwise-window-deaggregation}
  95:  0\le\log R(n)-\log A^*(n)\le\log2+h(n).
  96: \end{equation}
  97: For each exponent $e\ge2$, $\log e\le\sum_{2\le j\le e}\log j$, so
  98: \[
  99:  h(n)\le\sum_{\substack{p^j\mid n\\j\ge2}}\log j.
 100: \]
 101: The classical mean estimate \eqref{eq:mean-omega-input}, used uniformly with
 102: $\log\log(3y)$ in place of $\log\log y$ for small $y$, yields
 103: \begin{align*}
 104:  \sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)h(n)
 105:  &\le\sum_{\substack{p^j\le x\\j\ge2}}\log j
 106:        \sum_{m\le x/p^j}\omega(p^jm)\\
 107:  &\le\sum_{\substack{p^j\le x\\j\ge2}}\log j
 108:        \sum_{m\le x/p^j}(1+\omega(m))\\
 109:  &\ll x\log\log x\sum_p\sum_{j\ge2}\frac{\log j}{p^j}
 110:  \ll x\log\log x.
 111: \end{align*}
 112: The double series converges absolutely. Summing
 113: \eqref{eq:pointwise-window-deaggregation} and using
 114: $\sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)\ll x\log\log x$ proves
 115: \eqref{eq:mean-window-deaggregation}; the stated equivalence follows because
 116: $x\log\log x=o(x(\log\log x)^2)$.
 117: \end{proof}
 118: 
 119: \begin{corollary}[Single-rank sufficient and necessary interfaces]
 120: \label{cor:single-rank-mean-interfaces}
 121: A sufficient condition for \textup{(H2)} is
 122: \begin{equation}\label{eq:single-rank-sufficient-condition}
 123:  \sum_{d\le x}
 124:  \frac{(\omega(d)+\log\log x)\log\max\{1,a(d)\}}{d}
 125:  =o\bigl((\log\log x)^2\bigr).
 126: \end{equation}
 127: Conversely, \textup{(H2)} implies the diagonal necessary condition
 128: \begin{equation}\label{eq:single-rank-necessary-condition}
 129:  \sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)\log\max\{1,a(n)\}
 130:  =o\bigl(x(\log\log x)^2\bigr).
 131: \end{equation}
 132: \end{corollary}
 133: 
 134: \begin{proof}
 135: Since
 136: \[
 137:  \log A^*(n)\le\sum_{d\mid n}\log\max\{1,a(d)\},
 138: \]
 139: the substitution $n=dm$ and $\omega(dm)\le\omega(d)+\omega(m)$ gives
 140: \[
 141:  \sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)\log A^*(n)
 142:  \ll x\sum_{d\le x}
 143:  \frac{(\omega(d)+\log\log x)\log\max\{1,a(d)\}}{d}.
 144: \]
 145: Thus \eqref{eq:single-rank-sufficient-condition} and
 146: Theorem~\ref{thm:mean-rank-window-deaggregation} imply \textup{(H2)}. For the
 147: necessary condition, $n\in\mathcal D^+(n)$, and hence
 148: $R(n)\ge A^*(n)\ge\max\{1,a(n)\}$; summation proves the claim.
 149: \end{proof}
 150: 
 151: \begin{theorem}[Fibotomic rank entropy]
 152: \label{thm:fibotomic-rank-entropy}
 153: Let $\varphi=(1+\sqrt5)/2$, let $\phi_{\rm E}$ denote Euler's totient
 154: function, and, for $d\geq3$, put
 155: \[
 156:  \mathfrak F_d:=\prod_{r\mid d}F_r^{\mu(d/r)}.
 157: \]
 158: For $k\geq0$, set $m=\lfloor k/2\rfloor$ and
 159: \[
 160:  \mathcal H_d(k):=
 161:  k\log\frac{2d}{3}+2\log(m!)+(k-2m)\log(m+1).
 162: \]
 163: There is an absolute constant $C_F$ such that, for every $d\geq3$,
 164: \begin{equation}\label{eq:fibotomic-rank-entropy}
 165:  \mathcal H_d(a(d))\leq \phi_{\rm E}(d)\log\varphi+C_F.
 166: \end{equation}
 167: More generally, every finitely supported family of nonnegative real numbers
 168: $(\lambda_d)_{d\geq3}$ satisfies
 169: \begin{equation}\label{eq:weighted-fibotomic-rank-entropy}
 170:  \sum_{d\geq3}\lambda_d\mathcal H_d(a(d))
 171:  \leq (\log\varphi)\sum_{d\geq3}\lambda_d\phi_{\rm E}(d)
 172:        +C_F\sum_{d\geq3}\lambda_d.
 173: \end{equation}
 174: Uniformly as $d\to\infty$,
 175: \begin{equation}\label{eq:pointwise-exact-rank-bound}
 176:  a(d)\leq
 177:  \left(\frac{\log\varphi}{2}+o(1)\right)
 178:  \frac{\phi_{\rm E}(d)}{\log d}.
 179: \end{equation}
 180: Consequently, uniformly as $n\to\infty$,
 181: \begin{equation}\label{eq:visible-exact-rank-bound}
 182:  A^*(n)\leq
 183:  \left(\frac{\log\varphi}{2}+o(1)\right)
 184:  \frac{\phi_{\rm E}(n)}{\log n}.
 185: \end{equation}
 186: \end{theorem}
 187: 
 188: \begin{proof}
 189: Let $\Psi_d(x)$ be the $d$th fibotomic polynomial. The fibotomic
 190: factorization of Byer--Dvorachek--Eckard--Harrington--Wise--Wong
 191: \cite{CameronCountsLundMillerPiechnikWong2022Fibotomic} gives
 192: \[
 193:  F_d(x)=\prod_{r\mid d}\Psi_r(x),
 194:  \qquad
 195:  \mathfrak F_d=\Psi_d(1)\in\mathbb N.
 196: \]
 197: This published factorization is used as an input and is not asserted as a
 198: result of the present paper.
 199: Binet's formula, in the form
 200: \[
 201:  F_r=\frac{\varphi^r}{\sqrt5}
 202:  \bigl(1-(-\varphi^{-2})^r\bigr),
 203: \]
 204: and the identities
 205: $\sum_{r\mid d}\mu(d/r)=0$ and
 206: $\sum_{r\mid d}r\mu(d/r)=\phi_{\rm E}(d)$ show that
 207: \begin{equation}\label{eq:fibotomic-binet-error}
 208:  \log\mathfrak F_d=\phi_{\rm E}(d)\log\varphi+E_d,
 209:  \qquad
 210:  |E_d|\leq
 211:  C_F:=\sum_{j\geq1}
 212:  \left|\log\bigl(1-(-\varphi^{-2})^j\bigr)\right|.
 213: \end{equation}
 214: The series defining $C_F$ converges geometrically.
 215: 
 216: If $p\in\Pi_\alpha(d)$, then $p\mid F_d$ but $p\nmid F_r$ for every
 217: proper divisor $r$ of $d$. Since
 218: $F_d=\prod_{r\mid d}\mathfrak F_r$ and each factor is an integer, $p$ must
 219: divide $\mathfrak F_d$. Distinct exact-rank primes are coprime, and hence
 220: \begin{equation}\label{eq:exact-rank-radical-divides-fibotomic}
 221:  \prod_{p\in\Pi_\alpha(d)}p\mid\mathfrak F_d.
 222: \end{equation}
 223: For $p\notin\{2,5\}$, the classical rank congruence gives
 224: \[
 225:  d=\alpha(p)\mid p-\left(\frac5p\right),
 226: \]
 227: see \cite{Wall1960FibonacciModm,Vinson1963RankApparition}. Thus
 228: $p\equiv\pm1\pmod d$. The exceptional ranks $d=3,5$ have respectively
 229: the single exact-rank primes $2,5$ and satisfy the estimate directly. For
 230: every other $d$, write the exact-rank primes in increasing order as
 231: $p_1<\cdots<p_k$. There are at most two candidates in each pair of residue
 232: classes $jd\pm1$, whence
 233: \[
 234:  p_i\geq d\left\lceil\frac i2\right\rceil-1
 235:  \geq\frac{2d}{3}\left\lceil\frac i2\right\rceil.
 236: \]
 237: It follows that, with $m=\lfloor k/2\rfloor$,
 238: \begin{equation}\label{eq:rank-congruence-product-bound}
 239:  \prod_{i=1}^kp_i\geq
 240:  \left(\frac{2d}{3}\right)^k(m!)^2(m+1)^{k-2m}.
 241: \end{equation}
 242: Combining \eqref{eq:fibotomic-binet-error}--
 243: \eqref{eq:rank-congruence-product-bound} and taking logarithms proves
 244: \eqref{eq:fibotomic-rank-entropy}. Multiplication by $\lambda_d\geq0$ and
 245: finite summation proves \eqref{eq:weighted-fibotomic-rank-entropy}.
 246: 
 247: Stirling's lower bound applied in \eqref{eq:fibotomic-rank-entropy} gives,
 248: with an absolute implied constant,
 249: \begin{equation}\label{eq:rank-entropy-coarse}
 250:  a(d)\bigl(\log d+\log\max\{1,a(d)\}-O(1)\bigr)
 251:  \leq \phi_{\rm E}(d)\log\varphi+O(1).
 252: \end{equation}
 253: If $a(d)\leq\phi_{\rm E}(d)/(\log d)^2$, then
 254: \eqref{eq:pointwise-exact-rank-bound} follows immediately. Otherwise the
 255: standard uniform lower bound
 256: $\phi_{\rm E}(d)\gg d/\log\log(3d)$
 257: \cite{HardyWright2008Introduction} implies
 258: \[
 259:  \log a(d)=\log d+O(\log\log d)=\log d+o(\log d).
 260: \]
 261: Substitution in \eqref{eq:rank-entropy-coarse} yields
 262: $(2+o(1))a(d)\log d\leq\phi_{\rm E}(d)\log\varphi$, proving
 263: \eqref{eq:pointwise-exact-rank-bound} uniformly.
 264: 
 265: For \eqref{eq:visible-exact-rank-bound}, the case $A^*(n)=1$ follows from
 266: $\phi_{\rm E}(n)/\log n\to\infty$. Otherwise choose
 267: $d_n\in\mathcal D^+(n)$ with $A^*(n)=a(d_n)$. The preceding estimate has
 268: the coarse consequence $a(d)\ll d/\log(2d)$. If
 269: $d_n\leq n/(\log n)^3$, the lower bound for $\phi_{\rm E}(n)$ gives
 270: \[
 271:  a(d_n)=o\left(\frac{\phi_{\rm E}(n)}{\log n}\right).
 272: \]
 273: If $d_n>n/(\log n)^3$, then
 274: $\log d_n=(1+o(1))\log n$ and, since $d_n\mid n$,
 275: $\phi_{\rm E}(d_n)\leq\phi_{\rm E}(n)$. Applying
 276: \eqref{eq:pointwise-exact-rank-bound} at $d_n$ completes the proof.
 277: \end{proof}
 278: 

codex
The mathematical bottleneck is now clear. The requested `O(x(\log\log x)^2)` is weaker than the manuscript’s current `o(...)` interface, but it still requires an average bound for the maximum exact-rank multiplicity over visible divisors. Neither the existing fibotomic product bound nor Stroiński’s cumulative count appears to control that maximum. I’m now testing this against the primary papers and broader indexed searches, including whether a weighted/maximal-divisor theorem already supplies the missing step.

    <title>On big primitive divisors of Fibonacci numbers</title>

    <id>http://arxiv.org/abs/1606.01715v1</id>
    <title>On Dirichlet Products Evaluated at Fibonacci Numbers</title>
    <updated>2016-06-06T12:36:02Z</updated>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1606.01715v1" rel="alternate" type="text/html"/>

    <summary>In this work we discuss Dirichlet products evaluated at Fibonacci numbers. As first applications of the results we get a representation of Fibonacci numbers in terms of Euler's totient function, an upper bound on the number of primitive prime divisors and representations of some related Euler products. Moreover, we sum functions over all primitive divisors of a Fibonacci number and obtain a non--trivial fixed point of this operation.</summary>

  <entry>
    <id>http://arxiv.org/abs/1904.12393v2</id>
    <title>Primitive divisors of elliptic divisibility sequences over function fields with constant j-invariant</title>
    <updated>2019-12-22T18:06:31Z</updated>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1904.12393v2" rel="alternate" type="text/html"/>
    <link href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/1904.12393v2" rel="related" type="application/pdf" title="pdf"/>
    <summary>We prove an optimal Zsigmondy bound for elliptic divisibility sequences over function fields in case the $j$-invariant of the elliptic curve is constant. In more detail, given an elliptic curve $E$ with a point $P$ of infinite order, the sequence $D_1$, $D_2, \ldots$ of denominators of multiples $P$, $2P,\ldots$ of $P$ is a strong divisibility sequence in the sense that $\gcd(D_m, D_n) = D_{\gcd(m,n)}$. This is the genus-one analogue of the genus-zero Fibonacci, Lucas and Lehmer sequences. A number $N$ is called a Zsigmondy bound of the sequence if each term $D_{n}$ with $n&gt;N$ presents a new prime factor. The optimal uniform Zsigmondy bound for the genus-zero sequences over $\mathbf{Q}$ is $30$ by Bilu-Hanrot-Voutier, 2000, but finding such a bound remains an open problem in genus one, both over $\mathbf{Q}$ and over function fields. We prove that the optimal Zsigmondy bound for ordinary elliptic divisibility sequences over function fields is $2$ if the $j$-invariant is constant. In the supersingular case, we give a complete classification of which terms can and cannot have a new prime factor.</summary>
    <category term="math.NT" scheme="http://arxiv.org/schemas/atom"/>
    <category term="math.AG" scheme="http://arxiv.org/schemas/atom"/>

      <name>Bartosz Naskręcki</name>
    </author>

      <name>Marco Streng</name>
    </author>

As first applications of the results we get a representation of Fibonacci numbers in terms of Euler s totient function, an upper bound on the number of primitive prime divisors and representations of some related Euler products. arXiv:1606.01715v1 [math.NT] 6 Jun 2016  On Dirichlet Products Evaluated at Fibonacci
                                                               Numbers

                                                                         Uwe Stroinski
                                                                     November 12, 2018

                                                                                Abstract

                                             In this work we discuss sums of the form nx(f  g)(an) with an
                                         being the n­th Fibonacci number. As first applications of the results
                                         we get a representation of Fibonacci numbers in terms of Euler's ­
                                         function, an upper bound on the number of primitive prime divisors
                                         and a non­trivial fixed point h(m) = (n)=m h(n), where (n) is the
                                         least index m such that n|am.

                                         1 Introduction

                                         Let an denote the Fibonacci sequence, thus an is recursively given by a0 :=
                                         0, a1 := 1 and an+1 := an + an-1 for n  1. In the following we consider a
                                         version of the well­known formula from multiplicative number theory

                                             (f  g)(n) = f (n)G x = g(n)F x
                                                                n      n
                                         nx              nx        nx

                                         where G(x) := nx g(n) and F (x) := nx f (n) adapted to Dirichlet
                                         products evaluated at Fibonacci numbers.

                                             E-mail address: uwst@fa.uni-tuebingen.de. I thank Rainer Nagel for his valuable sup-
                                         port and the Tuebingen Functional Analysis group for their kind hospitality.

                                                             1
Theorem 1. Let f and g be arithmetic functions and let x  1 be real.
Then we have

    (f  g)(an) =         f (n)              g   ad·(n)        =              g(n)          f     ad·(n)  .
                                                  n                                                n
nx                (n)x          d        x                       (n)x              d    x
                                       (n)                                            (n)

Here  denotes the dual of the Fibonacci sequence (sometimes called the
rank of apparition) and is defined as

                              (n) := min{m  N : n|am},

the smallest integer m such that n|am.

I discuss the relation of this theorem to known results of Matiyasevich and
Guy [13] as well as results of Kiss [9] and give some further applications.
Among them are a new representation of Fibonacci numbers in terms of Eu-
ler's ­function, an upper bound for the number of primitive prime factors
in the Fibonacci sequence and explicit representations of some related Euler
products.

Let me fix some notation. Define

                            en := max{m  N : nm|a(n)},

the maximal power of n that still divides a(n). The integer part of a real

number x is x and the fractional part is {x}. Therefore x = x + {x}.

most of the results hold with exactly the same proofs for more general strong

divisibility sequences. Since we focus on Fibonacci numbers, we do not feel

the need to present each preliminary result in most general form. Especially

since it is Fibonacci numbers that have recently received attention (eg. a

polymath proposal concerning Littlewood's conjecture by Gowers [6]) and

since the problems are already considered hard for this special case (cf. eg.

Sarnak [15] on the number of Fibonacci primes or Granville [7] on the exis-

tence of Wall­Sun­Sun primes).

2 The proof of Theorem 1

In the next lemma we collect some results on the dual sequence.

Lemma 2. Let 0 < n, m  N and p be prime, then we have
    · duality: n|am if and only if (n)|m,
    · (pn) = pn-ep(p) for odd primes p with n > ep and
    · (2) = 3, (4) = 6 and (2n) = 3 · 2n-2 for n  3.
                                                3
The proof of this lemma is well­known and can be found e.g in Halton [8].

Let us now prove Theorem 1.

Proof. Let 1  x  R and n = x. For each x we consider a table with an

rows and n columns. Let 1  d  an and fill position (d, n) with f (d)g  an
                                                                       d

if (d)|n and with zero otherwise. This table is depicted in Figure 1.

Double counting yields

                 f (d)g an =           f (n)          g ad·(n) .
                             d                              n
     nx d:(d)|n                   nax         dx/(n)

For (n) > x we have     dx/(n) g  ad·(n)  = 0 and therefore the summation
                                      n

in the right sum can be restricted to those n  ax with (n)  x and since

n > ax implies (n) > x it suffices to sum over those n with (n)  x.

In the left sum we observe that d|an if and only if (d)|n. That proves the

first equality. The second equality in the theorem follows from f g = gf .

g d  ad·(n)  . In general, this is hard. However, one frequently consid-

4 An upper bound for the number of primitive

    primes

A primitive prime factor of an is a prime p that divides an but does not
divide am for 1  m  n - 1. The existence of primitive prime divisors for
Lucas numbers with positive discriminant and suffciently large index (with
Fibonacci numbers as a special case) is ensured by a result of Lucas [12] that
was later considerably generalized (cf. Carmichael [4], Schinzel [16], Bilu,
Hanrot and Voutier [2]).

Once existence is established it is natural to ask how many primitive prime
factors there are. We define

                                   (x) :=           1

                                              (p)x

as the number of primitive prime factors of Fibonacci numbers up to index
x and go on to show how Corollary 5 can be used to get an upper bound
complementing the existence results.

Theorem 7. We have

                       lim sup     log x  (x)       3 log r

There is still a gap between the lower bounds indicated by Carmichael's
                                               11
theorem and the upper bound given above. One might wonder why not to

directly prove a prime number theorem giving the exact asymptotics. The

difficulty here stems from the fact that current proofs of the prime number

theorem make use of nx (n)  px log p in a crucial way. In our

situation we have (n)x (n)  (p)x ep log p and (p)x log (p) =

(x) log x -  x  (t)   dt  by  partial  summation.  However, in the light of the
             1     t
                                                                          | pep
above estimate, |  (p)x ep log p -     (p)x log (p)| = |      (p)x  log          is
                                                                         (pep )

not asymptotically zero. The connection of the growth of (n)x (n) and

the growth of (x) is not as tight as in the situation of the prime number

theorem.

5 A representation of Fibonacci numbers

Let  be Euler's totient function. Since d|n (d) = n we obtain from
Theorem 1 that

                          (n)    x     =       an = ax+2 - 1
                               (n)
                   (n)x                    nx

where the last equality follows from an elementary induction argument.

Note that this can be used as an alternative definition of the Fibonacci num-
bers. To that purpose set a1 := 1 and define

                      ax+2 := 1 +                  x
                                             (n) (n)
                                       (n)x

for x  0. In that case  is also defined recursively, the sum is taken over all
numbers dividing at least one an with n  x.

Euler's totient function appears in many places in number theory. A connec-
tion to Fibonacci numbers like the above seems to be new.

on arithmetic functions.

Since f(2) = 0 we know that ­contraction as a linear operator on its
natural domain, the arithmetic functions, is not surjective. With 23 as a
nontrivial kernel element, ­contraction is not injective either.

7 The ­contraction of Liouville 

In this section we will compute . To that purpose we need a result of Cohn
[5] and independently by Wyler [19] ensuring that the only square Fibonacci
numbers are a1 = a2 = 1 and a12 = 144. This result was later generalized in
various directions cf. eg. London and Finkelstein [11], Lagarias and Weissel
[10], Ribenboim [14] and Bilu, Hanrot and Voutier [2].

As in the examples above we find that 1   is the characteristic function of
some set, namely the set of square numbers. Using the characterisation of

8 Euler products of ­contractions

In general ­contractions are not multiplicative and thus Euler products
might be hard to find. However, in the cases considered we can exploit
Corollary 3 and obtain e.g.

                         (n)                    (1     )(an)
                           ns                          ns
               (s)                  =                         =  1  +  2-s  +  12-s

                    n=1                   n=1

for s  C (as analytic extensions). Therefore

                         (n)
                           ns
                                    =     (1 +  2-s    +  12-s)     (1 - p-s)

                    n=1                                          p

                                                    20
for s =  + it with real part  > 1. With a similar argument we get

 µ(n)     =  (1 - 4-s)                    (1 - p-s),
      ns
n=1                     p>2

 µ2 (n)   =  (1 + 2-s + 3-s)                 (1 - p-s),
      ns
n=1                                       p

 µ3 (n)   =  (1 + 2-s + 3-s + 4-s)                       (1 - p-s)
      ns
n=1                                                   p

again for s =  + it with real part  > 1.

The fourth-order linear … Abstract The sets , , and of prime numbers defined by the 2-adic valuation of their Fibonacci rank being, respectively, 0, 1 and , are known to each have prime density . We discover Klein subgroups of generalized Laxton groups in which the sets of prime divisors of the three nonidentity linear recurrences of the Klein group are , , and . These Fibonacci-related recurrences are prime-known: we know all of their terms divisible by a given prime factor and the density of their prime factors. The fourth-order linear recurrence , where is the n -th Fibonacci number, is the first we study and is shown to be prime-known. Another source of prime-known recurrences comes from a density theorem proved by Cubre and Rouse . We derive prime-known Fibonacci-related linear recurrences from their theorem. Generalizations of the sets , , and associated with a general Lucas sequence are considered. Numerous remarks for further research are made. codex
A potentially relevant older trail surfaced in Google Scholar: Péter Kiss’s work on primitive divisors and “average order.” I’m checking the actual quantity there, because an average theorem for reciprocal sums or weighted primitive divisors would not imply the needed bound for `log #Π_α(d)`. I also found a 2025 Ballot paper, but its abstract concerns prime-density classes by the 2-adic valuation of the rank, not multiplicity of a fixed exact-rank fiber.

TITLE: Primitive divisors of Lucas numbers
SOURCE: Applications of Fibonacci numbers, Proc. 2nd Int. Conf., San JosÃ©/Ca., August 1986, 29-38 (1988).
REVIEW: [For the entire collection see Zbl 0635.00004.] Let \(A\) and \(B\) be fixed rational integers and \(R_ n=A R_{n-1}+B R_{n- 2}\) for \(n>2\), where the initial values are \(R_ 1 = 1\), \(R_ 2 = A\). Then \(R=\{R_ n\}^{\infty}_{n=1}\) is called a Lucas sequence. Let the roots of the characteristic polynomial \(f(x) = x^2 - Ax - B\) be \(\alpha\) and \(\beta\). We can assume \(| \alpha | > | \beta |\). The sequence \(R\) is said to be nondegenerate if \(AB \neq 0\), \(A^2 + 4B \neq 0\) and \(\alpha/\beta\) is not a root of unity. We say \(p^e\) \((e \geq 1)\) is a primitive prime power divisor of \(R_ n\) if \((p,B)=1\) and \(p\mid R_ n\) but \(p\nmid R_ m\) for \(m=1,2,\ldots,n-1\) and \(p^e \mid R_ n\) but \(p^{e+1}\nmid R_ n\). We denote \(R_ n = \prod p^e\) where the product is extended over all primitive prime power divisors of \(R_ n\). We write \(R_ n = 1\) if \(R_ n\) has no primitive prime divisor. Then the author proves the following: Theorem 1: If \(R\) is a nondegenerate Lucas sequence, then \[ \sum_{n\leq x} \log | R_ n| = \frac{3 \log |\alpha|}{\pi^2} x^2 + O(x \log x) \] where log \(| \alpha | > 0\) since \(| \alpha | > | \beta |\) and \(\alpha/\beta\) is not a root of unity so \(| \alpha | >1\). As a corollary he proves the following. If \(w(n)\) denotes the number of distinct prime factors of m, then \[ \sum_{n\leq x} w(R_ n) < \left(\frac{3 \log | \alpha |}{2\pi^2} + \varepsilon\right) \cdot \frac{x^2}{\log x} \] for any \(\varepsilon >0\) and \(x\geq x_ 0(\varepsilon)\). Let \(\text{PP}(R_ n)\) denote the greatest primitive prime power factors of Lucas numbers, then he proves: Theorem 2. Let \(x\) and \(\lambda\) \((0<\lambda <1)\) be real numbers. Let \(S_ x\) be the set of the terms \(R_ n\) of a nondegenerate Lucas sequence for which \(n \leq x\) and \(R_ n\) has a primitive prime power factor greater than \(n^{2-\lambda}\). Then we have \[ | S_ x| > \left(\frac{3\lambda}{2\pi^2}-\varepsilon\right)x \] for any \(\varepsilon < 0\) and \(x > x_ 0(\varepsilon)\) where \(S_ x\) denotes the cardinality of the set \(S_ x\).
REFS: 0

TITLE: On prime divisors of the terms of second order linear recurrence sequences
SOURCE: Applications of Fibonacci numbers. Vol. 3, Proc. 3rd Int. Conf., Pisa/Italy 1988, 203-207 (1990).
REVIEW: [For the entire collection see Zbl 0699.00017.] Let p(n) be the reciprocal sum of the primitive prime divisors of \(R_ n\), and f(n) the reciprocal sum of all prime divisors of \(R_ n\), where \((R_ n)\), \(n=0,1,2,..\). is a nondegenerate second order linear recurrence sequence. The author proves that: (1) \(\sum_{n\leq x}p(n)=\log \log x+O(1)\), if x is sufficiently large real number. (2) There is a constant \(c_ 0\), depending only on the sequence, such that \(\sum_{n\leq x}f(n)=c_ 0x+O(\log \log x)\) for any sufficiently large x.

TITLE: Some results concerning the reciprocal sum of prime divisors of a Lucas number
SOURCE: Bergum, G. E. (ed.) et al., Applications of Fibonacci numbers. Volume 5: Proceedings of the fifth international conference on Fibonacci numbers and their applications, University of St. Andrews, Scotland, July 20-24, 1992. Dordrecht: Kluwer Academic Publishers. 417-420 (1993).
REVIEW: The Lucas numbers with parameters \(A\), \(B\) are defined by the recurrence \(R_ n= AR_{n-1}+ BR_{n-2}\) \((n>1)\) with \(R_ 0=0\), \(R_ 1=1\), where \(A\), \(B\) are integers. By answering a question posed by P. ErdÅs, the author proves two interesting results, one of which says that for arbitrary small \(\varepsilon>0\) there exists a real number \(C>0\) such that \(\sum_{n\leq x} (\sum_{p\mid R_ n, p>C} {1\over p}) <\varepsilon x\) for sufficiently large \(x\) (with \(p\) denoting primes). For the entire collection see [Zbl 0781.00009].

TITLE: Existence of primitive divisors of Lucas and Lehmer numbers (with an appendix by M. Mignotte)
SOURCE: J. Reine Angew. Math. 539, 75-122 (2001).
REVIEW: A Lucas pair of algebraic numbers \((\alpha,\beta)\) is defined by the condition: \(\alpha+\beta\) and \(\alpha\beta\) are nonzero coprime rational integers and \(\alpha/\beta\) is not a root of unity. To such a pair \((\alpha,\beta)\) there corresponds a Lucas sequence defined by \(u_n(\alpha,\beta)= \frac{\alpha^n-\beta^n} {\alpha-\beta}\), \(n=0,1,2,\dots\;\). A Lehmer pair of algebraic numbers \((\alpha,\beta)\) is defined by the condition: \((\alpha+\beta)^2\) and \(\alpha\beta\) are nonzero coprime rational integers and \(\alpha/\beta\) is not a root of unity. The corresponding Lehmer sequence is defined by \(\widetilde{u}_n (\alpha,\beta)= \frac{\alpha^n-\beta^n} {\alpha-\beta}\) if \(n\) is odd and \(\widetilde{u}_n (\alpha,\beta)= \frac{\alpha^n-\beta^n} {\alpha^2-\beta^2}\) if \(n\) is even. Obviously, a Lucas pair is also a Lehmer pair with \(u_n= \widetilde{u}_n\) if \(n\) is odd and \(u_n= (\alpha+\beta) \widetilde{u}_n\) if \(n\) is even. There is a vast literature on Lucas and Lehmer sequences. One difficult question is the existence of primitive divisors for such sequences. A prime number \(p\) is a primitive divisor for the term \(u_N\) of a Lucas sequence \((u_n)\) if \(p\) divides \(u_n\) but does not divide \((\alpha-\beta)^2 u_1u_2\cdots u_{N-1}\). Analogously, a prime number \(p\) is a primitive divisor for the term \(\widetilde{u}_N\) of a Lehmer sequence \((\widetilde{u}_n)\) if \(p\) divides \(\widetilde{u}_N\) but does not divide \((\alpha^2-\beta^2)^2 \widetilde{u}_1 \widetilde{u}_2\cdots \widetilde{u}_{N-1}\). A Lucas (respectively, Lehmer) pair \((\alpha,\beta)\) is called \(N\)-defective Lucas (respectively, Lehmer) pair if \(u_N (\alpha,\beta)\) (respectively, \(\widetilde{u}_N (\alpha,\beta)\)) has no primitive divisor. A positive integer \(N\) is called totally non-defective if no \(N\)-defective Lehmer pair exists, i.e. if the \(N\)th term of every Lehmer sequence has a primitive divisor (hence, also, the \(N\)th term of every Lucas sequence has a primitive divisor). Main result of the paper: Every integer \(N> 30\) is totally non-defective. Based, on the other hand, on previous work of P. Voutier and an idea of C. Stewart, the authors explicitly classify all Lucas and Lehmer pairs which are \(N\)-defective for some \(N\leq 30\). As a result, the problem of listing explicitly all triples \((\alpha,\beta,N)\), where \((\alpha,\beta)\) is a Lucas (respectively, Lehmer) pair and \(N\) is a positive integer, is now completely solved due to this significant paper. The solution is, of course, too complicated to be described in a short review. We only mention a few important ingredients of the proof: (i) The cyclotomic criterion for Lehmer pairs due to C. Stewart. (ii) The method for explicitly solving by Baker's method Thue equations of very high degree, due to the first two authors of this paper. It is important to mention here that the method is adapted so that the knowledge of the full unit group is not necessary, but only a full rank subgroup of it. (iii) A variant by M. Mignotte of a theorem of M. Laurent, M. Mignotte and Y. Nesterenko on homogeneous forms in two logarithms of algebraic numbers. This variant deals with the more special linear form \(b_1i\pi- b_2\log\alpha\) and is given a separate 10 pages appendix. (iv) Heavy use of the computer and the computer package PARI for solving very many Thue equations, where the reduction of the very large upper bounds obtained by Baker's method is mainly achieved by the ``lemma of Baker and Davenport''.

TITLE: On Fibonacci numbers with few prime divisors
SOURCE: Proc. Japan Acad., Ser. A 81, No. 2, 17-20 (2005).
REVIEW: Three of these authors, \textit{Y. Bugeaud}, \textit{M. Mignotte} and \textit{S. Siksek} proved in an earlier paper [Classical and modular approaches to exponential Diophantine equations. II: The Lebesgue-Nagell equation, Compos. Math. 142, No. 1, 31--62 (2006; Zbl 1128.11013)] that \(F_n= y^m\), \(y\) a positive integer, \(m> 2\), has a solution only when \(F_n= 0,1,8\), and \(144\), and \(L_n= y^p\), \(y\) a positive integer, \(p\geq 2\), only when \(L_n= 1\) and \(4\). The proofs depend on the use of powerful techniques such as Baker's method and extensive computer verification. Using these results they show here that \(\omega(F_n)\geq 3\) for almost all \(n\) where \(\omega(m)\), \(m\) a positive integer, is the number of distinct prime divisors of \(m\), that is, Fibonacci numbers rarely have few prime factors. Further, \(\omega(F_n)\geq(\log n)^{\log 2+ o(1)}\) for almost all \(n\). They then conjecture that \(\omega(F_n)\gg\log n\) for composite \(n\) and support this with a heuristic argument.

TITLE: On big primitive divisors of Fibonacci numbers
SOURCE: Ramanujan J. 67, No. 1, Paper No. 20, 19 p. (2025).
REVIEW: Let \(\{F_n\}\) denote the sequence of Fibonacci numbers, and let \(\kappa\) be a positive integer. From Stewart's work on the lower bound for the greatest prime divisor of Lucas numbers, it follows that there exists a positive integer \(n_0(\kappa)\) such that, for all \(n \geq n_0(\kappa)\), \(F_n\) has a primitive divisor not smaller than \((\kappa+1)n - 1\). In this paper, the author explicitly computes the value of \(n_0(\kappa)\). The main tool employed is an estimate for \(p\)-adic logarithmic forms.

TITLE: On the divisibility of the rank of appearance of a Lucas sequence
SOURCE: Int. J. Number Theory 18, No. 10, 2145-2156 (2022).
REVIEW: Let \( \{U_n\}_{n\ge 0} \) be a \textit{Lucas sequence}, that is, a sequence of integers satisfying the linear recurrence: \( U_0=0 \), \( U_1=1 \), and \( U_n=a_1U_{n-1}+a_2U_{n-2} \) for all integers \( n\ge 2 \), where \( a_1 \) and \( a_2 \) are fixed nonzero integers. The \textit{rank of appearance} of a prime number \( p \), denoted by \( \rho_U(p) \), is the smallest positive integer \( k \) such that \( p|U_k \). It can be easily seen that \( \rho_U(p) \) exists whenever \( p\not| ~a_2 \). Define \[ \mathcal{R}_U(d;x):=\#\{p\le x~:~p\not| ~a_2, ~d|\rho_U(p)\}, \] for every positive integer \( d \) and for every \( x>1 \). Let \( \alpha \) and \( \beta \) be the roots of the characteristic polynomial \( f_U(X)=X^2-a_1X-a_2 \), and assume that \( \gamma:=\alpha/\beta \) is not a root of unity. Let \( \Delta:=a_1^2+4a_2 \) be the discriminant of \( f_U(X) \), and let \( \Delta_0 \) be the squarefree part of it. Given an integer \( d \), let \( d^{\infty} \) denote the supernatural number \( \prod_{p|d}p^{\infty} \). In the paper under review, the author proves the following theorem, which is the main result in the paper. Theorem 1. Let \( d \) be an odd positive integer with \( 3\nmid~d \) whenever \( \Delta_0=-3 \). There exists an absolute constant \( B>0 \), such that for every \( x>\exp (Bd^{40}) \) we have \[ \mathcal{R}_U(d;x)=\delta_U(d)\text{Li}(x)+O_U\left(\dfrac{d}{\varphi(d)}\cdot \dfrac{x(\log \log x)^{\omega(d)}}{(\log x)^{\frac{9}{8}}}\right), \] where \[ \delta_U(d):=\frac{1}{d}\left(\dfrac{1}{(d^{\infty},h)}+\eta_U(d)\right)\prod_{p|d}\left(1-\frac{1}{p^2}\right)^{-1}, \] with \( \eta_U(d):=0 \) if \( \Delta>0 \) or \( \Delta_0 \not\equiv 1~(\text{mod}~4) \) or \( \Delta_0\not|~d^{\infty} \); and \[ \eta_U(d):=\dfrac{(d_\infty, d)}{[(d^\infty, h), \Delta_0/(d, \Delta_0)]^2} \] otherwise. The proof of Theorem 1 follows from a clever combination of techniques in analytic and algebraic number theory, the usual asymptotic properties of the Lucas sequences, and the well-known results on the subject in the literature.

\section{Further questions}\label{sec:questions}
%% ====================================================================

The preceding sections complete the divisor-lattice theorem chain for this
article.  We record only two boundary markers; neither is used as evidence for
the results proved above.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*)]
\item Classify connected support kernels of size at least four. By the
\suppref{Corollary on four-support reduction}, the one-, two-, and
three-coordinate classifications already determine every disconnected
four-coordinate pattern. The \suppref{Corollary ``Theta-refined connected
support entropy''} shows that connected kernels already realize its lower bound in
the fixed-choice rank-pure minimal-cover sector, so a full connected
high-support classification is not a lower-order residual problem within that
sector. It would be a separate project.
\item Prove or disprove either rank-window sparsity conjecture. For the mean
hypothesis, Theorem~\ref{thm:mean-rank-window-deaggregation} reduces the
problem to the visible single-rank maximum
\eqref{eq:visible-single-rank-interface}; Proposition
\ref{prop:blms-obstructs-mean-window} also shows that a published conjecture
predicts failure rather than sparsity. As explained in
Remark~\ref{rem:exact-average-open-interface}, the remaining question is an
upper distribution problem for exact-rank prime multiplicities.
The paper derives conditional normal and mean orders, but does not
claim that either conjecture follows from presently known primitive-divisor
theorems, RH, or ERH.
\end{enumerate}

============================================================
1. Overall assessment: Major revision
The principal structural resultsâ€”especially the minimal-generator criterion, witness-cover bijection, and connected-block factorizationâ€”appear mathematically correct. I found no counterexample to the theorem chain, and I independently reproduced all values of  $A(n)$  and  $M_n$  for  $2\le n\le30$ , as well as

$$M_{42}=\{52,58,211,1684,12209\}.$$

The manuscript is nevertheless not ready for acceptance. Two matters are blocking:


The novelty boundary is not adequately established. Several advertised theorems are nearly formal consequences of classical strong divisibility, rank-lcm identities, and elementary irredundant-cover combinatorics.


The claimed â€œsharp minimal-generator growthâ€ is sharp only after separating the uncontrolled arithmetic quantity  $R(n)$ . The actual normal and mean orders remain conditional on hypotheses constructed precisely to make the error term negligible.


For a specialist venue such as The Fibonacci Quarterly, the paper is potentially publishable after substantial reframing and compression. At a broader number-theory journal, the present theorem package would probably be judged insufficiently deep.
2. Novelty ratings
The preview Theorems Aâ€“D duplicate the numbered results and are not rated separately.

4. Missing or insufficiently discussed references


StroiÅ„ski is cited, but his explicit cumulative primitive-prime estimate is not discussed. His Theorem 7 gives an upper bound for

$$\pi_\alpha(x)=\#\{p:\alpha(p)\le x\},$$

directly relevant to Remark 6.2. See StroiÅ„ski, On Dirichlet Products Evaluated at Fibonacci Numbers.


Marc Renault proves the lcm identity for ranks in the more general  $(a,b)$ -Fibonacci setting. This is important to the novelty assessment of Theorems 2.2, 3.3 and 5.2: Renault, The Period, Rank, and Order of the  $(a,b)$ -Fibonacci Sequence Mod  $m$ .


The modern valuation treatment of Lengyelâ€™s formulas should be cited alongside the 1995 paper: Medinaâ€“Rowland,  $p$ -regularity of the  $p$ -adic valuation of the Fibonacci sequence.


The primitive-divisor discussion should include Granville, Primitive prime factors in second-order linear recurrence sequences and Kiss, Primitive Divisors of Lucas Numbers.


For the current arithmetic context around sizes of primitive divisors, cite Hong, On big primitive divisors of Fibonacci numbers. This does not prove (H1), but it helps delimit what modern primitive-divisor results actually control.


The computational discussion should acknowledge the classical factorization literature, notably Brillhartâ€“Montgomeryâ€“Silverman, Tables of Fibonacci and Lucas Factorizations, Math. Comp. 50 (1988), 251â€“260.


5. Specific improvements needed for acceptance


Reorganize the paper around genuinely new endpoints: the witness-cover bijection, support-three classification, minimal connected factorization, and combinatorial entropy bound.


State the structural results first in a general strong-divisibility setting. This would turn their formal nature into a conceptual theorem rather than an unacknowledged limitation.


Reframe Section 6 so that â€œsharpâ€ always modifies support-type entropy, not the unknown arithmetic growth of  $\#M_n$ .


Add unconditional consequences currently omitted: an almost-everywhere lower bound and a maximal-order lower bound along primorial indices.


Repair Proposition 2.5, Corollary 3.8, the primitive-divisor terminology, and the support-three uniqueness statement.


Supply the complete computational archive.


Remove internal editorial language and reduce the manuscript substantially; approximately 20â€“25 pages plus a computational appendix would be more appropriate.


6. Concrete fixes for all BLOCKER and MEDIUM issues
B1: Extract the general structural theorem
A suitable replacement for much of Â§Â§3 and 5 is the following.

General strong-divisibility fiber theorem.
Let  $(u_n)_{n\ge1}$  be a positive strong divisibility sequence with

$$u_1=1,\qquad \gcd(u_a,u_b)=u_{\gcd(a,b)}.$$

For every  $q$  dividing some term, put

$$\alpha_u(q)=\min\{r\ge1:q\mid u_r\}.$$

Then

$$q\mid u_m\iff \alpha_u(q)\mid m,
\qquad
\alpha_u(\operatorname{lcm}(a,b))
=\operatorname{lcm}(\alpha_u(a),\alpha_u(b)).$$

Consequently, for

$$B_n(u)=\{q\mid u_n:\alpha_u(q)=n\},$$

one has

$$B_n(u)=\operatorname{Div}(u_n)
\setminus\bigcup_{p\mid n}\operatorname{Div}(u_{n/p}).$$

If  $m=\prod_i p_i^{e_i}\mid u_n$ , then  $m$  is minimal in  $B_n(u)$  iff

$$\operatorname{lcm}_i\alpha_u(p_i^{e_i})=n$$

and, for every  $i$ ,

$$\operatorname{lcm}\!\left(
\alpha_u(p_i^{e_i-1}),
\{\alpha_u(p_j^{e_j}):j\ne i\}\right)<n.$$


The duality proof is short: if  $q\mid u_{\alpha(q)}$  and  $q\mid u_m$ , then

$$q\mid\gcd(u_{\alpha(q)},u_m)=u_{\gcd(\alpha(q),m)}.$$

Minimality of  $\alpha(q)$  forces  $\gcd(\alpha(q),m)=\alpha(q)$ .
The witness-cover and connected-component statements can then be proved in this general theorem. The Fibonacci-specific contribution begins only when the prime-power valuation formulas imply the primitive/ladder dichotomy. This separation would make the novelty intellectually transparent.
B2: Correct the growth claims
The unconditional statement should be presented exactly as

$$\frac{\log2}{4}\omega(n)^2-O(1)
\le \log\#M_n
\le
\frac{\log2}{4}\omega(n)^2
+\omega(n)\log R(n)+O(\omega(n)).$$

The sentence following it should say:

The coefficient  $(\log2)/4$  is the exact entropy constant for the support combinatorics. This is not an asymptotic formula for  $\log\#M_n$  unless  $R(n)=\exp(o(\omega(n)))$ .

Then add two genuinely unconditional corollaries.
From Hardyâ€“Ramanujan,

$$\omega(n)=(1+o(1))\log\log n$$

for almost all  $n$ , hence

$$\log\#M_n\ge
\left(\frac{\log2}{4}+o(1)\right)(\log\log n)^2
\quad\text{for almost all }n.$$

For the primorial sequence  $N_y=\prod_{p\le y}p$ ,

$$\omega(N_y)\sim\frac{\log N_y}{\log\log N_y},$$

and therefore

$$\log\#M_{N_y}\ge
\left(\frac{\log2}{4}+o(1)\right)
\frac{(\log N_y)^2}{(\log\log N_y)^2}.$$

These are meaningful unconditional growth theorems. Hypotheses (H1) and (H2) should then be labelled conjectures, and (6.8)â€“(6.9) conditional corollaries.
M1: Quantify the known baseline for  $R(n)$ 
Every prime atom occurring in  $A_{I,J}(n)$  divides  $F_n$ , while a fixed ladder family contributes at most one atom. Thus

$$R(n)\le 1+\omega(F_n).$$

Since

$$2^{\omega(F_n)}\le\operatorname{rad}(F_n)\le F_n$$

and  $\log F_n=n\log\varphi+O(1)$ ,

$$\log R(n)\le \log n+O(1).$$

This should be stated before (H1). It also demonstrates the strength of the conjecture: for almost all  $n$ , (H1) asks for

$$\log R(n)=o(\log\log n),$$

whereas the elementary bound gives only  $O(\log n)$ .
StroiÅ„skiâ€™s cumulative estimate should then be quoted:

$$\limsup_{x\to\infty}
\frac{\log x}{x^2}\,
\#\{p:\alpha(p)\le x\}
\le \frac{3\log\varphi}{2\pi^2}.$$

Explain explicitly that this is also far too weak to imply (H1).
M2: Repair Proposition 2.5
Insert the missing step:
For  $p\ne2,5$ , the classical congruence

$$F_{p-(5/p)}\equiv0\pmod p$$

implies

$$z_p=\alpha(p)\mid p-\left(\frac5p\right).$$

Therefore  $p\nmid z_p$ . Lengyelâ€™s formula gives, whenever  $z_p\mid r$ ,

$$\nu_p(F_r)=\nu_p(F_{z_p})+\nu_p(r).$$

Writing  $r=z_pu$  now yields

$$\nu_p(F_{z_pu})=h_p+\nu_p(u).$$

Hence  $p^e\mid F_{z_pu}$  iff

$$\nu_p(u)\ge e-h_p,$$

and the least admissible  $u$  is

$$u=p^{\max(e-h_p,0)}.$$

This proves

$$\alpha(p^e)=z_pp^{\max(e-h_p,0)}$$

without a hidden assumption.
M3: Separate the primitive notions
Use the following terminology consistently:


Prime atom of rank  $d$ : a prime  $p$  with  $\alpha(p)=d$ .


Ladder atom: an atomic prime power  $p^e$  with  $e>1$ .


Primitive divisor of  $F_d$ : a prime satisfying the explicitly adopted Carmichael/BHV convention.


Then replace â€œprimitive familyâ€  $P_J(n)$  by â€œexact-rank prime slotâ€ unless every member is primitive under the declared convention. In particular, treat  $p=5$  at  $d=5$  as an exceptional exact-rank prime if the BHV discriminant-excluding definition is adopted.
M4: Correct Corollary 3.8
The admissible pair universe should be reduced to

$$\mathcal P_k=
\{(I,J):\varnothing\ne I\subseteq J\subseteq[k],
\ I=J\text{ or }|I|=1\}.$$

Its cardinality is

$$N'_k=(2^k-1)+k(2^{k-1}-1).$$

A correct statement is:

Every minimal generator with  $\omega(n)=k$  determines a set

$$T(m)\subseteq\mathcal P_k,\qquad |T(m)|\le k,$$

satisfying coverage and private-coordinate irredundance. Consequently the number of possible labelled support types is at most

$$\sum_{r=1}^{k}\binom{N'_k}{r}.$$

The converse need not hold: arithmetic realization additionally requires nonempty atom families and a system of representatives with distinct underlying primes.

This removes the false â€œsubject only toâ€ claim and strengthens the numerical bound.
M5: Correct the support-three uniqueness claim
The precise statement should be:

Each  $m\in M_n$  determines a unique labelled atomic factor set

$$\{(\theta,E_n(\theta),T_n(\theta)):\theta\mid m\}.$$

Its unlabelled support shape is the unique  $S_3$ -orbit  $\Gamma_i$  containing the labelled support set. Enumeration over all coordinate permutations may list the same labelled row or product more than once because of stabilizers; the final result is the set of distinct products.

For the algorithm, either:


insert products into a mathematical set, or


replace each labelled row by the lexicographically least member of its  $S_3$ -orbit.


Do not claim uniqueness of the raw permuted enumeration.
M6: Supply a reproducible computational supplement
The archive should contain:


compute_birth_layer_table.py;


verify_deepening_delta.py;


the exact factorization data used through  $n=210$ ;


generated TeX/TSV tables;


the complete verification log;


Python and dependency versions;


SHA-256 hashes.


The verification should compare the actual sets

$$B_n^{\rm direct}=B_n^{\rm upper\ fiber},
\qquad
M_n^{\rm direct}=M_n^{\rm witness}$$

rather than only their cardinalities. Until that material is part of the submission, Section 7 should not claim reproducibility.
In summary, the manuscript contains a sound and potentially publishable structural core, but acceptance requires a more honest theorem hierarchy: general structural facts first, Fibonacci-specific atom arithmetic second, and the unresolved  $R(n)$  problem clearly separated from the proved combinatorial entropy.

## Novelty Boundary and Residual Interface

The new order statement is combinatorial-arithmetic: after grouping atomic
prime powers by their essential/full support pair, the witness-cover private
coordinates give the exact entropy constant `log(2)/4`. Unconditionally,

`log #M_n >= (log(2)/4) omega(n)^2 + O(1)` as `omega(n) -> infinity`,

and the matching upper bound differs only by
`omega(n) log R(n)`, where `R(n)` is the maximum cardinality of one atomic
rank window. Consequently the paper proves the exact normal and mean orders
under explicitly stated normal-density and mean rank-window sparsity
hypotheses. No located paper contains this witness-cover entropy theorem.

The remaining arithmetic statement, `log R(n)=o(omega(n))` for almost all
`n` (or its mean analogue), is not a consequence of Carmichael/BHV, the
prime-power lifting formulas, Hardy--Ramanujan, TurÃ¡n, ErdÅ‘s--Wintner, RH, or
ERH as presently known. Carmichael/BHV supplies at least one primitive prime
in each nonexceptional rank; it gives no upper distribution law for the number
of primitive primes in the rank windows. The manuscript therefore labels
this precise multiplicity assertion as an open interface and does not present
it as proved.

## Follow-up Check for the Fibotomic Rank-Entropy Bound

Checked 2026-08-03. The arXiv Atom API was queried for fibotomic, rank of
apparition AND Fibonacci, order of appearance AND Fibonacci, primitive prime
divisors AND Fibonacci, number of primitive divisors AND Fibonacci, prime
factors AND fibotomic, and primitive part AND Fibonacci. Crossref and OpenAlex
were queried independently for the corresponding bibliographic phrases. No
returned record states the pointwise estimate
\[
 a(d)\leq\left(\frac{\log\varphi}{2}+o(1)\right)
 \frac{\phi_{\rm E}(d)}{\log d},
\]
the fibotomic entropy inequality from which it follows, the induced bound for
the visible maximum \(A^*(n)\), or either weighted estimate for
\(\sum\omega(n)\log A^*(n)\).

The full texts of arXiv:2009.03345 and arXiv:1606.01715 were checked.
Byer--Dvorachek--Eckard--Harrington--Wise--Wong prove the fibotomic
polynomial factorization used as an input, but do not derive an exact-rank
prime-count entropy bound. StroiÅ„ski's Theorem 7 proves only the already-cited
cumulative estimate
\[
 \limsup_{x\to\infty}\frac{\log x}{x^2}
 \#\{p:\alpha(p)\leq x\}
 \leq\frac{3\log\varphi}{2\pi^2}.
\]
That cumulative conclusion remains attributed to StroiÅ„ski and is not claimed
as a new theorem here.

Jarden's original one-page article was retrieved from the Fibonacci Quarterly
archive and read in full. Its exact metadata are D. Jarden, *Any Lucas Number
\(L_{5p}\), for Any Prime \(p>5\), Has at Least Two Distinct Primitive Prime
Divisors*, *The Fibonacci Quarterly* 6 (1968), no. 6, 407, DOI
10.1080/00150517.1968.12431197. The paper supplies the two primitive Lucas
prime divisors; the present manuscript separately proves that each has
Fibonacci rank \(10p\) before using it in the weighted lower bound.

The absence of a match in these searches is not a proof of global priority.
It is the stated evidence for the novelty classification used in this
revision.

 succeeded in 209ms:
   1: \begin{theorem}[Combinatorial support entropy]
   2: \label{thm:minimal-generator-growth}
   3: For every integer $n\ge3$, put $k=\omega(n)$ and
   4: $L(n)=\log\#\mathcal M_n$. Then $\mathcal M_n$ is nonempty. If $k\ge3$ and
   5: $r=\lfloor k/2\rfloor$, then
   6: \begin{equation}\label{eq:private-cover-finite-bounds}
   7:  (2^r-1)^{k-r}
   8:  \le \#\mathcal M_n
   9:  \le \sum_{s=1}^{k}\binom{k}{s}(2R(n))^s2^{s(k-s)}.
  10: \end{equation}
  11: In particular, uniformly along every sequence for which $\omega(n)\to\infty$,
  12: \begin{equation}\label{eq:private-cover-entropy-order}
  13:  \frac{\log2}{4}\,\omega(n)^2-O(1)
  14:  \le L(n)
  15:  \le \frac{\log2}{4}\,\omega(n)^2
  16:       +\omega(n)\log R(n)+O(\omega(n)).
  17: \end{equation}
  18: The coefficient $\log2/4$ is the exact quadratic coefficient of the
  19: multiplicity-free minimal-cover contribution: the lower construction embeds
  20: the unweighted cover count, and the private-coordinate encoding gives the same
  21: quadratic support term in the upper bound. This is not an asymptotic formula
  22: for $L(n)$ unless $R(n)=\exp(o(\omega(n)))$; no such unconditional estimate
  23: for $R(n)$ is proved here. In particular, the displayed coefficient is not
  24: claimed to be optimal among all deductions from fibotomic or exact-rank
  25: arithmetic information.
  26: 
  27: For comparison, the crude support-count estimate gives
  28: \begin{equation}
  29: \label{eq:finite-minimal-generator-bound}
  30:  \#\mathcal M_n

  22: we use the convention $B_2=\mathcal M_2=\varnothing$ and $A(2)=0$.
  23: Throughout, $\log$ is the natural logarithm, $\omega(t)$ is the number of
  24: distinct prime divisors of $t$, and $\Omega(t)$ counts prime divisors with
  25: multiplicity. We write $A_{\rm pr}(n)$ for the number of primes of exact rank
  26: $n$, $\mathsf{Part}(X)$ for the set of set partitions of a finite set $X$,
  27: and $\mathsf B_k$ for the $k$th Bell number. A hypergraph on one vertex is
  28: regarded as connected. These conventions also apply when the notation first
  29: appears in a later section.
  30: The counting function $A(n)$ is subsumed by the
  31: $\alpha$-contraction formalism of Stroi\'nski \cite{Stroinski2016AlphaContraction};
  32: see also the classical M\"obius inversions discussed in
  33: \cite{Halton1966DivisibilityFibonacci,FultonMorris1969ArithmeticalFunctions}.
  34: In this paper we go beyond enumeration and study $B_n$ as an
  35: ordered subset of the divisor lattice $(D_n,\mid)$, asking how it sits inside
  36: $D_n$, how it is generated, and what combinatorial structure it carries.
  37: 
  38: Three classical facts underlie the analysis. First, $q\mid F_m$ if and only if
  39: $\alpha(q)\mid m$
  40: \cite{Wall1960FibonacciModm,Vinson1963RankApparition}. Second, the Fibonacci
  41: sequence satisfies strong divisibility: $\gcd(F_a,F_b)=F_{\gcd(a,b)}$.
  42: Third, ranks of apparition respect least common multiples:
  43: $\alpha(\lcmop(u,v))=\lcmop(\alpha(u),\alpha(v))$; Renault proves the
  44: corresponding identity in the more general $(a,b)$-Fibonacci setting
  45: \cite{Renault2013PeriodRankOrder}. These properties, combined with the
  46: primitive divisor theorem for Fibonacci numbers
  47: \cite{Carmichael1913PrimitiveDivisors,BiluHanrotVoutier2001PrimitiveDivisorsLucas},
  48: are the sole inputs for the main results.
  49: 
  50: This point of view complements several nearby strands in the literature.
  51: Luca and Pomerance \cite{LucaPomerance2014LocalBehavior} study the local
  52: behavior of $\alpha$ under additive shifts; Trojovsk\'a
  53: \cite{Trojovska2020PeriodicPoints} and FitzGibbons--Javaheri--Miller--Verga
  54: \cite{FitzGibbonsJavaheriMillerVerga2025Dynamics} study periodic points and
  55: iteration dynamics of $\alpha$; Byer--Dvorachek--Eckard--Harrington--Wise--Wong
  56: \cite{CameronCountsLundMillerPiechnikWong2022Fibotomic} study fibotomic
  57: polynomials and related algebraic factorizations; and Sanna
  58: \cite{Sanna2016LucasSequences} and Marques \cite{Marques2012OrderAppearance}
  59: study the growth and fixed-point behavior of the order-of-appearance map. The
  60: present paper instead fixes a single fiber $B_n$ and studies its divisibility
  61: geometry inside $D_n$. The upper-fiber identity, the coordinatewise minimal-lcm
  62: criterion, the prime--ladder alternative, the private-coordinate injection, and
  63: the coprimality of disjoint coordinate blocks are recorded below as lemmas:
  64: they are formal consequences of the cited classical identities and are not
  65: claimed as independent arithmetic advances. The contributions developed from
  66: these facts are the witness-cover formulation, the exhaustive support-three
  67: classification with its arithmetic realization test, the passage of connected
  68: block factorization to minimal elements, and the support-entropy coefficient
  69: $\log 2/4$. A further contribution is the support-preserving rank-pure
  70: embedding of arbitrary minimal set covers, which transfers the classical theta
  71: asymptotic and a discrete central local limit to an explicit Fibonacci sector.
  72: These lower results isolate, rather than resolve, the arithmetic multiplicity
  73: $R(n)$. Accordingly, we make no unconditional claim of a sharp asymptotic for
  74: $\#\mathcal M_n$ or of the negligibility of its disconnected part. We do,
  75: however, isolate the mean interface further: support-window aggregation costs
  76: only $O(x\log\log x)$ on average, so only a visible single-rank maximum remains.
  77: A fibotomic size estimate and the congruence restrictions on exact-rank primes
  78: then yield a uniform entropy inequality for each exact-rank class and a
  79: pointwise bound of order $\phi_{\rm E}(d)/\log d$. This improves the available
  80: single-rank input but does not settle the mean sparse-window hypothesis.
  81: A conditional dominance theorem also identifies the stronger blockwise control
  82: that would make the disconnected part exponentially negligible. The mean
  83: sparse-window hypothesis is not presented as a prediction: Conjecture~5.1 of
  84: Bugeaud--Luca--Mignotte--Siksek
  85: \cite{BugeaudLucaMignotteSiksek2005FewPrimeDivisors} would imply its failure.

+## Independent Recheck for the Weighted Visible-Maximum Target
+
+Checked 2026-08-08. This recheck queried the arXiv Atom API (up to 100
+records per query), Crossref, Google Scholar, and zbMATH Open, and inspected
+the full text and reference list of arXiv:1606.01715 together with Crossref's
+46-item reference list for Bilu--Hanrot--Voutier. Searches covered Fibonacci
+rank/order of appearance, primitive-divisor counts and average orders,
+fibotomic prime factors, and exact-rank multiplicities.
+
+The reference-chain check found an important predecessor to state explicitly.
+Kiss, *Primitive Divisors of Lucas Numbers* (1988), proves an asymptotic for
+the accumulated logarithmic mass of primitive parts and deduces the cumulative
+`x^2/log x` upper-bound scale for the number of distinct primitive primes.
+Stroinski's Theorem 7 gives the explicit limsup constant on that scale. A
+second Kiss paper (1990) averages reciprocal sums of primitive divisors; it is
+not an average theorem for their number. The manuscript now credits this
+boundary explicitly.
+
+Bilu--Hanrot--Voutier proves existence for all Lucas and Lehmer indices above
+30 and classifies the defective cases below 31. In the present Fibonacci
+argument it supplies existence of one exact-rank prime at the required
+nonexceptional indices. It supplies no upper distribution law for
+`a(d) = #Pi_alpha(d)`.
+
+No located source proves, or readily implies,
+`sum_{n<=x} omega(n) log A*(n) = O(x (log log x)^2)`. In particular, Kiss and
+Stroinski control cumulative counts or logarithmic mass, Sanna counts primes
+whose rank is divisible by a fixed integer rather than primes of one exact
+rank, and modern large-primitive-divisor results control the size of at least
+one primitive divisor rather than the multiplicity of an exact-rank class.
+The missing input is an unconditional upper-distribution estimate for
+`log #Pi_alpha(d)` along divisor maxima; the primitive-part size and
+congruence restrictions alone do not provide it.

+## Independent Recheck for the Weighted Visible-Maximum Target
+
+Checked 2026-08-08. This recheck queried the arXiv Atom API (up to 100
+records per query), Crossref, Google Scholar, and zbMATH Open, and inspected
+the full text and reference list of arXiv:1606.01715 together with Crossref's
+46-item reference list for Bilu--Hanrot--Voutier. Searches covered Fibonacci
+rank/order of appearance, primitive-divisor counts and average orders,
+fibotomic prime factors, and exact-rank multiplicities.
+
+The reference-chain check found an important predecessor to state explicitly.
+Kiss, *Primitive Divisors of Lucas Numbers* (1988), proves an asymptotic for
+the accumulated logarithmic mass of primitive parts and deduces the cumulative
+`x^2/log x` upper-bound scale for the number of distinct primitive primes.
+Stroinski's Theorem 7 gives the explicit limsup constant on that scale. A
+second Kiss paper (1990) averages reciprocal sums of primitive divisors; it is
+not an average theorem for their number. The manuscript now credits this
+boundary explicitly.
+
+Bilu--Hanrot--Voutier proves existence for all Lucas and Lehmer indices above
+30 and classifies the defective cases below 31. In the present Fibonacci
+argument it supplies existence of one exact-rank prime at the required
+nonexceptional indices. It supplies no upper distribution law for
+`a(d) = #Pi_alpha(d)`.
+
+No located source proves, or readily implies,
+`sum_{n<=x} omega(n) log A*(n) = O(x (log log x)^2)`. In particular, Kiss and
+Stroinski control cumulative counts or logarithmic mass, Sanna counts primes
+whose rank is divisible by a fixed integer rather than primes of one exact
+rank, and modern large-primitive-divisor results control the size of at least
+one primitive divisor rather than the multiplicity of an exact-rank class.
+The missing input is an unconditional upper-distribution estimate for
+`log #Pi_alpha(d)` along divisor maxima; the primitive-part size and
+congruence restrictions alone do not provide it.

---
name: verification-before-completion
description: Use when about to claim work is complete, fixed, or passing, before committing or creating PRs - requires running verification commands and confirming output before making any success claims; evidence before assertions always
---

# Verification Before Completion

## Overview

Claiming work is complete without verification is dishonesty, not efficiency.

**Core principle:** Evidence before claims, always.

**Violating the letter of this rule is violating the spirit of this rule.**

## The Iron Law

```
NO COMPLETION CLAIMS WITHOUT FRESH VERIFICATION EVIDENCE
```

If you haven't run the verification command in this message, you cannot claim it passes.

## The Gate Function

```
BEFORE claiming any status or expressing satisfaction:

1. IDENTIFY: What command proves this claim?
2. RUN: Execute the FULL command (fresh, complete)
3. READ: Full output, check exit code, count failures
4. VERIFY: Does output confirm the claim?
   - If NO: State actual status with evidence
   - If YES: State claim WITH evidence
5. ONLY THEN: Make the claim

Skip any step = lying, not verifying
```

## Common Failures

| Claim | Requires | Not Sufficient |
|-------|----------|----------------|
| Tests pass | Test command output: 0 failures | Previous run, "should pass" |
| Linter clean | Linter output: 0 errors | Partial check, extrapolation |
| Build succeeds | Build command: exit 0 | Linter passing, logs look good |
| Bug fixed | Test original symptom: passes | Code changed, assumed fixed |
| Regression test works | Red-green cycle verified | Test passes once |
| Agent completed | VCS diff shows changes | Agent reports "success" |
| Requirements met | Line-by-line checklist | Tests passing |

## Red Flags - STOP

- Using "should", "probably", "seems to"
- Expressing satisfaction before verification ("Great!", "Perfect!", "Done!", etc.)
- About to commit/push/PR without verification
- Trusting agent success reports
- Relying on partial verification
- Thinking "just this once"
- Tired and wanting work over
- **ANY wording implying success without having run verification**

## Rationalization Prevention

| Excuse | Reality |
|--------|---------|
| "Should work now" | RUN the verification |
| "I'm confident" | Confidence â‰  evidence |
| "Just this once" | No exceptions |
| "Linter passed" | Linter â‰  compiler |
| "Agent said success" | Verify independently |
| "I'm tired" | Exhaustion â‰  excuse |
| "Partial check is enough" | Partial proves nothing |
| "Different words so rule doesn't apply" | Spirit over letter |

## Key Patterns

**Tests:**
```
âœ… [Run test command] [See: 34/34 pass] "All tests pass"
âŒ "Should pass now" / "Looks correct"
```

**Regression tests (TDD Red-Green):**
```
âœ… Write â†’ Run (pass) â†’ Revert fix â†’ Run (MUST FAIL) â†’ Restore â†’ Run (pass)
âŒ "I've written a regression test" (without red-green verification)
```

**Build:**
```
âœ… [Run build] [See: exit 0] "Build passes"
âŒ "Linter passed" (linter doesn't check compilation)
```

**Requirements:**
```
âœ… Re-read plan â†’ Create checklist â†’ Verify each â†’ Report gaps or completion
âŒ "Tests pass, phase complete"
```

**Agent delegation:**
```
âœ… Agent reports success â†’ Check VCS diff â†’ Verify changes â†’ Report actual state
âŒ Trust agent report
```

## Why This Matters

From 24 failure memories:
- your human partner said "I don't believe you" - trust broken
- Undefined functions shipped - would crash
- Missing requirements shipped - incomplete features
- Time wasted on false completion â†’ redirect â†’ rework
- Violates: "Honesty is a core value. If you lie, you'll be replaced."

## When To Apply

**ALWAYS before:**
- ANY variation of success/completion claims
- ANY expression of satisfaction
- ANY positive statement about work state
- Committing, PR creation, task completion
- Moving to next task
- Delegating to agents

**Rule applies to:**
- Exact phrases
- Paraphrases and synonyms
- Implications of success
- ANY communication suggesting completion/correctness

## The Bottom Line

**No shortcuts for verification.**

Run the command. Read the output. THEN claim the result.

This is non-negotiable.

+## Independent Recheck for the Weighted Visible-Maximum Target
+
+Checked 2026-08-08. This recheck queried the arXiv Atom API (up to 100
+records per query), Crossref, Google Scholar, and zbMATH Open, and inspected
+the full text and reference list of arXiv:1606.01715 together with Crossref's
+46-item reference list for Bilu--Hanrot--Voutier. Searches covered Fibonacci
+rank/order of appearance, primitive-divisor counts and average orders,
+fibotomic prime factors, and exact-rank multiplicities.
+
+The reference-chain check found an important predecessor to state explicitly.
+Kiss, *Primitive Divisors of Lucas Numbers* (1988), proves an asymptotic for
+the accumulated logarithmic mass of primitive parts and deduces the cumulative
+`x^2/log x` upper-bound scale for the number of distinct primitive primes.
+Stroinski's Theorem 7 gives the explicit limsup constant on that scale. A
+second Kiss paper (1990) averages reciprocal sums of primitive divisors; it is
+not an average theorem for their number. The manuscript now credits this
+boundary explicitly.
+
+Bilu--Hanrot--Voutier proves existence for all Lucas and Lehmer indices above
+30 and classifies the defective cases below 31. In the present Fibonacci
+argument it supplies existence of one exact-rank prime at the required
+nonexceptional indices. It supplies no upper distribution law for
+`a(d) = #Pi_alpha(d)`.
+
+No located source proves, or readily implies,
+`sum_{n<=x} omega(n) log A*(n) = O(x (log log x)^2)`. In particular, Kiss and
+Stroinski control cumulative counts or logarithmic mass, Sanna counts primes
+whose rank is divisible by a fixed integer rather than primes of one exact
+rank, and modern large-primitive-divisor results control the size of at least
+one primitive divisor rather than the multiplicity of an exact-rank class.
+The missing input is an unconditional upper-distribution estimate for
+`log #Pi_alpha(d)` along divisor maxima; the primitive-part size and
+congruence restrictions alone do not provide it.
